## What I did

I audited the keyless coding path end-to-end (not just source review), then fixed the two concrete defects the audit surfaced. Both are on the existing branch `fix/forge-start-script` → **PR #23** (open, mergeable, 2 files, CI green on push and pull_request).

### Fix 1 — broken `npm start` (`package.json`)
`"start": "node server.js"` referenced a file that isn't in the repo. `npm start` failed with exit 1 (`Cannot find module server.js`). Repointed it at the real entrypoint `tsx server.ts`, matching the existing `"server"` script. Verified: `npm start` → exit 0, `/api/health` → `ONLINE`.

### Fix 2 — stale verification matrix (`src/App.tsx`)
The in-app Acceptance Criteria tab still claimed the model path needed a `GEMINI_API_KEY` (AC4/AC5) and that PR creation was `BLOCKED` (AC11). Both contradict the keyless-primary requirement and observed reality. I corrected AC4, AC5, AC11, AC12, AC18 to the evidence. Lint + build pass.

## Audit findings — what's strong, what's weak

**Working (verified by me, first-hand):**
- Keyless E2E genuinely runs. I dispatched `forge.yml` on current `main` (run `38243201821`): provider **`ovh` / Meta-Llama-3.3-70B**, `fallbackUsed:false`, no API key, isolated patch to `src/types/agent.ts`, `npm install`/`lint`/`build`/`git diff --check` all exit 0, commit `0e9ce57`, **PR #25 opened by the Actions bot**.
- Failover works: when Kilo returned `429`, freelm fell through to OVH automatically. This is exactly your requirement — the agent finds the finest free model available without keys.
- Safety boundaries are real: exact-command registry with `shell:false`, protected-path/traversal/symlink checks, secret stripping from child processes, and server-side token validation with an allowlist.

**Weak / worth your attention (I did not change these without your call):**
1. **One-shot keyless routing, no explicit preference.** The agent tries `chat → chat:fast → chat:small` and takes whatever answers first; there's no preference for the strongest available model. "Finest available" currently means "first that responds."
2. **Gemini fallback is dead code in keyless mode.** `FIRSTBORN_KEYLESS_ONLY=true` deletes credentials and disables Gemini entirely — fine for keyless purity, but there is no configured secondary route.
3. **`issues:[labeled]` trigger has no server-side auth.** The workflow gate uses `author_association == 'OWNER'`, which is decent, but any OWNER-labeled issue body flows straight into the model prompt.
4. **`verify.yml` PR runs on `forge/task-*` branches show `action_required` (0 jobs).** A repo settings gate, not a code bug — but it means PRs opened by the agent don't get an automatic green check.
5. **No README.** The GPT/controller handoff contract lives only in `COORDINATION.md` and `CLAUDE_AUDIT_PROMPT.md`; there's no single entry doc for an external collaborator.
6. **Model context is bounded to ~5 files / 16k chars.** Good for free-tier limits, but it will refuse (BLOCKED) on larger tasks.

## My recommendation for "ultimate agent for shared GPT"

Your stated model goal — *three long-context, keyless, always-find-the-best-free-model* — is partly met but not engineered for it. The highest-value next change would be a **routing layer** that ranks candidate free models by capability/length and only falls back on genuine failure, rather than first-response-wins. I deliberately did **not** implement that now, because it's an architecture change and you should approve the approach first.

Would you like me to (a) build that capability-ranked keyless router, (b) add a `README.md` that formalizes the GPT controller handoff (task schema, evidence contract, status semantics), or (c) fix the `action_required` PR-check gate? Say which and I'll do it on a fresh branch.

## What I built (branch `feat/forge-controller-hardening`, PR #34)
https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/pull/34 — OPEN, mergeable, 2 files, CI green.

### (a) Capability-first keyless routing — `src/services/model.ts`
The concrete defect: the loop was `['chat', 'chat:fast', 'chat:small']`. I read `freelm`'s internals and confirmed `chat` resolves to the provider's **whole** chat pool (where a small model answers first), while `chat:large`/`chat:best` resolve to the large-tagged models. The old code **never requested the top tier**, so it could not meet your "finest free model available" requirement.

Now Forge enumerates the live candidate set, requests `chat:large`/`chat:best` **first**, degrades only on failure/no-text, and reports which stronger routes were skipped in `keylessRouteErrors`. Verified with a strict-keyless probe:
- `chat:large` → **`nvidia/nemotron-3-super-120b-a12b:free` (120B) via kilo** — stronger than the old `chat`-first pick (`poolside/laguna-s-2.1:free`).
- `ModelService.generatePlanAndPatch` probe → `SUCCESS: true`, kilo, 120B, `fallbackUsed:false`.

### (b) Controller-handoff `README.md` (new)
The integration contract for a shared-GPT controller: the Actions workflow + HTTP task interfaces (with real inputs/paths/endpoints I read from `forge.yml` and `server.ts`), the keyless capability-first model policy, the `TaskDefinition`/`TaskExecutionReport` contracts, status semantics (`FAILED` vs `BLOCKED`), the evidence contract, the safety model, and the known external blockers.

### (c) PR-check gate — a truth I have to be straight about
I could not "build" this one. I probed the GitHub API directly: the token is a **GitHub App installation token**, which returns **403 on `/actions/permissions`** — it cannot read or change repo Actions settings. Clean tree, no stray files. Here is the verified result.

## You were right — and here is the actual cause

Forge (your app) kept failing missions, and the error it reported was misleading. It said routes returned **"no usable text"**. The real mechanism, reproduced locally with no provider keys:

- Forge's **strongest** keyless route is `chat:large` → kilo `nvidia/nemotron-3-super-120b-a12b:free`. That model is a **reasoning model**.
- Forge sends `max_tokens: 1024`. The model spends the *entire* budget on hidden `reasoning_content` and returns **empty text with `finish_reason=length`**.
- Forge logs "no usable text", drops to weaker models (Meta-Llama-3.3-70B, Mistral-Small), which return truncated/incomplete JSON → patch validation/`TEST` fails → mission dies.

Direct experiment:

| request | result |
|---|---|
| `max_tokens=1024` (before) | kilo/nemotron → `len=0`, `finish=length`, 4575 chars hidden reasoning |
| `max_tokens=1024` + `reasoning_effort=none` (after) | kilo/nemotron → `len=355`, `finish=stop` ✅ |

**On Groq:** there is no Groq route in the app. `GROQ_API_KEY` only appears in the list of credential names that keyless mode *deletes*. The live keyless providers are `kilo` and `ovh` only — so Groq was never requested, consistent with what you said.

## Fix (one file, minimal)

`src/services/model.ts` — in the alias failover loop:
- send `reasoning_effort: 'none'` so the compact JSON answer fits the 1024-token budget;
- on empty text, record `finish_reason` and the concrete model (was a generic message), so this class of failure is diagnosable instead of cryptic.

No architecture, secrets, permissions, or unrelated files changed.

## Verification (actual commands)

| command | exit | note |
|---|---|---|
| `npm run lint` | **0** | tsc clean |
| `npm run build` | **0** | |
| `npm test` | **0** | 43/43 pass |
| `FIRSTBORN_KEYLESS_ONLY=True npm run model:smoke` | **0** | `provider=kilo`, `nvidia/nemotron-3-super-120b-a12b:free`, `responseValidated=true`, `attemptCount=1` |

I dispatched a **real keyless coding mission on the fixed branch** (run [38379384074](https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/actions/runs/38379384074)):

- trace: `INSPECT:PASSED → PLAN:PASSED → VALIDATE:PASSED → APPLY:PASSED → TEST:PASSED → POLICY:PASSED → COMMIT:PASSED → PR:PASSED`
- route that ran: `kilo / nvidia/nemotron-3-super-120b-a12b:free`, **callsCount: 1**, `fallbackUsed: false` — the previously-failing top-tier route now answers
- result: `status: PR_OPENED` — **Forge opened [PR #47](https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/pull/47) by itself** (added `docs/forge-keyless-route-fix.md`)

So the connected controller ("Shared GPT") should now be able to finish missions, because the model route no longer dies on the first call.

Your real requirement: **Forge obtains working credentials without you in the loop.** There are exactly two legitimate patterns, and I verified the first one live just now.

- needs **no key**, so it doesn't break your constraint;

- must reuse the `reasoning_effort: 'none'` + bounded-retry handling we already built.

Option A is the direct fix for the blocker we proved: the run sits at `action_required` purely because the PR author is `github-actions[bot]`, and a single `POST .../actions/runs/{id}/approve` flips it to `success` (we saw exactly that on PR #52).

Option B closes the *substantive* gap that `verify` runs `npm run model:smoke` but Forge's own internal gate only runs `lint`, `test`, `build`. Add `model:smoke` to that internal list, and even if CI approval ever breaks, the mission itself has run **every documented check** and recorded it in the evidence artifact.

## The one tradeoff, stated plainly

Auto-approving defeats a control GitHub added on purpose (2026-06-11) so bot-generated code can't auto-run workflows. For your repo it's low-risk because `verify` is `contents: read` with **no secrets** — but I won't pretend it's free. If you'd rather not relax it, Option B alone still gives you a fully autonomous, fully-checked path; you just lose the green badge.

## The one thing I must verify before promising it

Whether the workflow's own `GITHUB_TOKEN` (with `actions: write` added) is allowed to approve the run — GitHub may restrict a token from approving runs in its own workflow chain. If it can't, we fall back to B, which is guaranteed. I'd test it on a throwaway PR before calling it done, not assert it.

All three are small and workflow-only. Want me to go ahead? If you only want one, do **#2 first** — it's the guaranteed win and needs no permission gamble.

- missing repo (404) → `REPO_INSPECTION / FIX_INPUTS / retryable=false` ← *this one found a real bug: 404s previously fell through to `UNKNOWN`*

**Retry gate, proven live:** with `FORGE_MAX_ATTEMPTS=3` and a non-retryable failure, no re-run was emitted — it refused to loop.

**Safety rails:** never retries `VALIDATION`/`POLICY`/`REPO_INSPECTION`/`NO_SUBSTANTIVE_CHECKS`/`UNKNOWN`; hard cap 3 attempts; stops if a branch already exists so it can't open a duplicate PR; recovery actions are advisory and never auto-executed.

## 2. Forge audit now catches this class — PR opened

- Wired into `verifyFinding` **and** as a proactive HIGH verified fact in `runCompanyAudit`, so the crash is reported even when no lens claims it.

- **Evidence:** lint 0; **259/259 tests**; build 0; **`model:smoke` PASS** (keyless route: `kilo` / `nvidia/nemotron-3.5-super-vl:free`, valid response).

## Judgment calls (14) — opinions, explicitly not proven
**HIGH**

- **Missing Environment Variable Validation** — `server.ts`, `.env.example`: `GEMINI_API_KEY` is used with only a null check → add schema validation (e.g. Zod).
- **Insecure Express Configuration** — `server.ts`: `express.json({ limit: '15mb' })` allows large payloads (DoS) → lower the limit, add rate limiting.

**MEDIUM**
- **Tight Coupling between Server and Business Logic** — `server.ts`: intent detection + fallback responses live in the entrypoint → extract a `TutorService`.
- **Inconsistent Environment Variable Handling** — `server.ts`, `vite.config.ts`: no centralized config/validation → add one.
- **Unsafe Server-Side Proxy Endpoint** — `server.ts`: `POST /api/chat` accepts arbitrary body without schema validation → validate inputs.
- **Potential SSRF/Open Redirect via Proxy** — `server.ts`: proxy lacks strict origin validation → add CORS + rate limiting.

- **Inconsistent TypeScript Configuration** — `tsconfig.json`, `package.json`: `noEmit` set while `lint` runs `tsc --noEmit` → the build can't catch type errors.
- **Hardcoded Dev/Prod Environment Logic** — `vite.config.ts`: manual `DISABLE_HMR` toggle → use Vite modes.

Here's what's actually broken on Amina `main` (`bf95762`) right now — all found by `tsc` + reading the code, none of them in the audit's report:

## Real defects the audit missed

**1. P0 — NOUR crashes to a blank screen when no lesson resolves**
`teacherContext.targetLesson` is typed `OfficialCurriculumLesson | null`, and `VirtualTeacherClassroom.tsx` renders it **without a null guard**:
- line 1333: `{teacherContext.targetLesson.subjectNameAr}`
- line 1698: `{teacherContext.targetLesson.subjectNameAr}`

Same class in `teacherContextEngine.ts` (lines 763–844): `lesson.exercises`, `lesson.objectives` dereferenced when `lesson` can be `null`. If the curriculum has no matching lesson (empty/missing lesson id, or a day-record subject that maps to nothing), the teacher screen throws `Cannot read properties of null`. This is the flagship screen.

**2. P1 — Nour silently receives empty school context**
`CompanionChatScreen.tsx` (lines 390–398) builds the "verified school context" with field names that **don't exist on the types**:
- `currentDayRecord.lessons` → the real field is `lessonsCovered`
- `currentDayRecord.homework` → the real field is `homeworkAssigned`
- `activeMission.subjectId` → the real field is `subject`

`server.ts` (lines 122–127) stringifies that object straight into Nour's system prompt as authoritative context. So `dayRecord.lessons`, `dayRecord.homework`, and `activeMission.subjectId` are all `undefined` — Nour answers with *less* context than the app actually holds, and never sees today's lessons, homework, or the mission's subject.

**3. P1 — invalid evidence modality corrupts the mastery record**
`missionCompletion.ts` line 118 emits `modality: 'practice'`, but `EvidenceModality = 'quiz' | 'game' | 'reel_check' | 'homework'` (`types/mastery.ts:14`). `practice` isn't a legal value, so evidence written from a practice session carries a type the rest of the system doesn't define.

These are exactly the "app is full of [problems]" you're sensing — and they're all invisible to the company audit's file-existence checks.

I'd fix these three on the existing Amina branch (`fix/nour-handoff-undefined-setter`) — they're the same theme: the Nour/context path is wired wrong. Concretely:
- guard `targetLesson` (render an honest "no lesson" state instead of crashing) and the `lesson` derefs in `teacherContextEngine`;
- correct the field names in `CompanionChatScreen` so Nour actually gets the lessons/homework/subject;
- map `practice` to a legal `EvidenceModality` (or add it to the union if that's intended).

## The order they actually run

1. **Fetch:** the audit downloads the file **inventory** (all 130 paths) but only **22 file excerpts**, each **truncated to 4,000 characters** (`repo-context.ts:57-70`).
2. **Lenses read:** the four auditors are handed that sample, not the repo. The prompt literally says: *"The excerpts are a representative sample, not the whole repository. Do NOT report 'an excerpt was not provided'… omit a finding instead."*
3. **Report:** the lenses write findings from that sample; evidence checks then ask only "does the cited path exist."

So no — they never read line by line. They read a **sample**.

## The numbers

| | |
|---|---|
| Amina source files | **120** |
| Files the audit read | **22** (alphabetical + entrypoints) |
| Per-file cut | **4,000 chars** |
| Code actually seen | **~88 KB of ~1,862 KB = 4.7%** |
| The `CompanionChatScreen` bug | at byte **19,877** — ~5× past the 4,000-char cut |

And three of the four real bug files were **never even sampled**:

| File | Read by the audit? |
|---|---|
| `src/components/companion/CompanionChatScreen.tsx` | sampled, but truncated before line 390 |
| `src/services/teacher/teacherContextEngine.ts` | **never read** |
| `src/services/missions/missionCompletion.ts` | **never read** |
| `src/components/teacher/VirtualTeacherClassroom.tsx` | **never read** |

## So what is the company for, right now?

It's a **plausible-sounding opinion generator over 5% of the code**. Worse, the prompt *instructs* the lenses to omit gaps — so they filled the silence with generic architecture/security advice (Zod, rate limiting, CSP, "split the god module"). That's exactly why every finding feels like something you already knew and none of them is why the app is broken.

The one real hit it got — the missing lockfile — was readable because `package.json` is tiny and sits in the "entrypoints" list, so it fit inside 4,000 chars. Everything bigger than a few KB was effectively invisible.

**The company isn't useless — it's underfed.** It was designed as a cheap, read-only sample-and-judge, and that design cannot find behavioral defects. To do what you're asking (read everything, understand, *then* find the solution), it needs two changes:

1. **Read every file in full** — no 22-file cap, no 4,000-char cut. Feed the whole corpus to the lenses in batches (map-reduce: per-file/per-area passes, then merge), so every line is actually read before any conclusion.
2. **Run the target's own real checks as evidence** — `tsc --noEmit`, its tests, its build — in isolation. That single step surfaces all 15 of Amina's type errors automatically. This is exactly the #85 "isolated runtime verification" work, and it's a deliberate execution/security decision (the audit currently promises *"no target code executed"*).

That's a real architectural change to the audit (bigger prompts, batching, and executing untrusted code under isolation), and it costs tokens — which you've said to watch.

My recommendation: let me do it in two steps —
- **Step 1 (safe, read-only):** make the audit read **all** files in **full**, batched, so coverage goes 4.7% → 100%.
- **Step 2 (isolated execution):** add the target's `lint`/`test`/`build` as first-class evidence under the #85 sandbox.

| R2 | Honest provider/model reporting | **PASS** | Report records real `provider`/`model`/`fallbackUsed` |
| R3 | Reads real target source before patching | **PASS** | Fetches up to 5 real files + manifest from target branch |

---

## 3. Confirmed defects, ranked

### HIGH

**H1 — `npm start` is broken on `main`.** `package.json:9` → `"start": "node server.js"`, but `server.js` is never built (no esbuild step wired; `clean` deletes it). Reproduced: exit 1, `MODULE_NOT_FOUND`. A fix already exists in **unmerged PR #23** (`"start": "tsx server.ts"`), so `main` is knowingly broken.
*Impact:* the documented production start command fails.

**H2 — Hard capability ceiling prevents "complicated missions."** `src/services/model.ts:83` and `:124` instruct the model to fit **≤2 files / ≤3 edits** and cap output at **1024 tokens**; `forge.ts:36` bounds source context to 24k chars and `:324` rejects files >16k. This is a deliberate trade-off for free-tier models, but it means anything beyond a small bounded edit (multi-file refactor, cross-module feature, migration) will be refused or truncated.
*Impact:* Forge is reliable only for small, single-concern patches. "No problems whatsoever on complicated missions" is **not** achievable at this commit.

**H3 — No criterion-specific evidence.** `forge.ts:242-245` overwrites every requirement status to `PENDING` with a generic "target-check passed" string. Forge cannot prove a specific requirement was met, only that some generic checks passed.
*Impact:* a reviewer cannot trace acceptance criteria to proof — directly contradicts AGENTS.md rule 2.

### MEDIUM

**M1 — `targetBranch` is silently ignored.** `server.ts:104,117` accept and pass it; `forge.ts`/`forge-runner.ts` never read it. Inspection, clone, and PR base always use the repo default branch. *Impact:* tasks aimed at a non-default branch silently run against the wrong branch.

**M2 — Runtime fragility.** Live evidence during this audit: `chat via kilo: response contained no usable text` and `429 rate limit exceeded` on `thinkingmachines/inkling-small:free`. `maxAttempts:12` + 3 aliases usually recovers, but success depends on anonymous endpoints outside your control. *Impact:* intermittent FAILED runs with no code cause.

**M3 — UI misreports the active model.** `src/App.tsx` header hardcodes `"Gemini 2.5 Flash"` while the real primary is the keyless freelm router. *Impact:* operator-facing dishonesty about which model ran.

**M4 — UI acceptance matrix is hand-asserted.** `App.tsx:560-620` hardcodes `PASS` badges not derived from live evidence (PR #23 partially corrects this but is unmerged).

### LOW

- **L1 —** `git diff --check` is appended to checks (`forge.ts:537`) but is vacuous unless the worktree has edits; harmless but adds no assurance.
- **L2 —** `vite.config.ts:11` uses `__dirname`; Vite warns it breaks under the future native config loader.
- **L3 —** `package-lock.json` untracked → installs non-reproducible and the `npm ci` branch in `forge.yml:54` never executes.

---

## 4. Security verdict

The security posture is **strong**, and I found no exploitable hole:

- **Command execution** (`test-runner.ts`): fixed allowlist, `spawn` with `shell:false`, minimal env (`PATH/HOME/LANG/TZ/TMPDIR`). No injection surface.
- **Path safety** (`forge.ts:68-107, 146-205`): traversal, absolute-path, symlink, and protected-path (`.git`, `.env`, `.github`, `package.json`, configs, `scripts/`) guards are correct.
- **Secrets:** `GITHUB_TOKEN`/`GEMINI_API_KEY` are stripped from the git subprocess env; token is injected only as a git extraheader. No secrets reach the model.
- **API** (`server.ts`): `requireGitHubToken` validates against GitHub and enforces a login allowlist; repo allowlist on both endpoints.
- **Workflow** (`forge.yml`): gated on owner-authored `forge-task` label; scoped `contents: write` + `pull-requests: write`.

Remaining risk is **operational, not exploitable**: prompt injection from untrusted issue text is mitigated only by the model prompt and the allowlist, and the write-capable token lives in the job. Acceptable for owner-gated triggers.

---

## 5. Bottom line

- **Model smoke test: PASS** ✅
- **Real coding mission (single-concern): PASS** ✅ — I independently proved a full intake → source read → patch → isolated apply → checks → commit → PR cycle (PR #28).
- **Safe & reliable for routine, small missions: YES** ✅
- **Reliable for *complicated* multi-file missions: NO** ❌ — blocked by H2/H3, plus the H1 start-command break on `main`.

So, to answer your question directly: Forge **is** genuinely functional and safe for bounded work, but it is **not** ready to "work on complicated missions without any problems whatsoever" at this commit. The blockers are structural (capability caps + no per-criterion evidence), not mysterious bugs.

---

## 6. Recommended next action (one)

**Merge the already-open PR #23** (fixes H1 `npm start` + corrects the false UI model/acceptance claims), then raise the capability ceiling for complicated missions (H2) and add per-requirement evidence (H3) on a dedicated branch.

## Part 2 — On giving the agent "all permissions / ultimate control"

I have to be straight with you, and I want to be useful rather than just say no.

**First, a technical fact:** I cannot grant or change repository permissions from here. My connected GitHub integration returns `403 Resource not accessible by integration` for the Actions-permissions endpoint. So this is not me refusing to click a button I have — the admin capability genuinely isn't in this integration's scope.

**Second, the actual gap.** Forge is blocked from exactly one thing: **GitHub Actions creating pull requests** (`HTTP 403: GitHub Actions is not permitted to create or approve pull requests`). That's a repository setting, not code.

**The safe fix — one toggle, least privilege:**

> Settings → Actions → General → Workflow permissions → enable **"Allow GitHub Actions to create and approve pull requests"**, keeping **"Read repository contents and packages permissions"** as the default.

Because `forge.yml` already declares scoped `permissions: contents: write` + `pull-requests: write`, this is enough to let Forge create its own PRs — and it stays narrow.

**What I deliberately did not do, and recommend against:**
- Turning the *repository default* to "Read and write" (that would silently let `verify.yml` and every other workflow write).
- Adding `admin`, `secrets`, `actions`, `deployments`, `packages`, or `attestations`.
- Removing the owner-login and repo allowlists.
- Auto-merging `main`. Forge correctly pushes only to `forge/task-*` branches and leaves merge to a human.

Giving an autonomous agent "all distinct permissions" over a repository that also holds your **secrets** is the one thing that turns "a bad mission" into "a compromised account." The three fixes above plus that one toggle give Forge **full operational parity** for coding missions — healthy, but still bounded.

**What I need from you (pick one):**
1. Flip that one setting yourself (recommended), or
2. Confirm explicitly that you want me to also widen workflow scope, and I'll do it on a new branch with the exact permissions documented — I still won't enable admin/secret-management.

---

**Bottom line:** Code fixes are done and verified in PR #29. Forge can now run small/medium coding missions end-to-end; the only thing between it and autonomous PR creation is the one repository setting above, which needs your click. The remaining capability ceiling for *very complicated* missions (H2: ≤2 files / ≤3 edits / 1024-token cap) is a design trade-off for free-tier models — tell me if you want me to raise it and I'll do that as a separate, tested PR.

**[11:51:42] Watcher/agent:**

Short answer: **the time was model retries against a rate-limited free tier, not the checks.** I measured it.

## Where the time actually goes

| Step | Time |
|---|---|
| `npm install` | 0.65s |
| `npm run lint` | ~1.0s |
| `npm test` | 1.9s |
| `npm run build` | 1.0s |
| `git diff --check` | 0.02s |
| **All five checks** | **~4.5s** |

So the checks are basically free. **Everything else is waiting on model calls.**

## Why the waits were so long

1. **The free tier is rate-limited.** OVH allows ~2 requests/min *per model*; Kilo ~3/min *shared*. Under load, calls queue and time out with "no answer within the call's deadline."
2. **A slow model was second in my chain.** `Qwen3.8-27B` measured **27 seconds** for a trivial reply — so nearly every mission stalled on it.
3. **Retry fan-out.** freelm was set to `maxAttempts: 12` with `hedge: true`, and I'd added a second full pass. That multiplied attempts.
4. **My test method.** I ran 3–5 missions *sequentially*, so one "batch" was 10–25 minutes of wall-clock — which read as "forever."

## What I changed

- **Reordered the chain by measured speed**: `Mistral-Small` (~1.1s) → `Llama-70B` (~2.0s) → `gpt-oss-20b` (~6.9s) → slower ones last.
- **Cut fan-out**: `maxAttempts` 12 → 6, `hedge` off.
- **Per-call timeout** 45s → 20s.
- **Added a hard sweep budget** (`FORGE_MODEL_BUDGET_MS`, default 45s) so a saturated tier fails in ~1.5 min instead of grinding for 6.
- **Removed the second pass** that doubled the sweep.

## Measured result

| Version | One full mission |
|---|---|
| Before | **5m42s**, then failed |
| Intermediate | 2m37s |
| **Final** | **1m32s** |

## The honest part

The slowness *was* the failure — it was grinding a saturated free tier for minutes and often failing anyway. Now it degrades quickly and tells the truth (`keyless model routes unavailable`) instead of hanging.

But a fast failure is still a failure. **The only way to make it fast *and* reliable is a real key.** Groq's free tier answers in well under a second, and `model.ts` already uses it automatically if `GROQ_API_KEY` is set. That single input turns this from "1.5 min, coin-flip" into "seconds, dependable."

Want me to wire a `FORGE_MODEL_CHAIN` default tuned to Groq and add a quick `model:smoke` check so you can verify the key the moment you drop it in?

**[11:54:59] OWNER (verbatim):**

> yeah but the API key will exhausted right away we already have Gemini and and I can provide a grok API key so

**[11:59:57] Watcher/agent:**

You're right that a single key runs dry — so I didn't just wire one key, I made the layer **spread load across providers and models**. Here's the key insight from reading the installed freelm source:

## Quotas are per *model*, not per key

| Provider | Free limit | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| Groq | **30 rpm / 1000 rpd** | per model |
| Gemini | ~20 rpd **per model**, 1M context | per model |
| OVH (keyless) | ~2 rpm | per model |
| Kilo (keyless) | ~3 rpm | shared |

So one Groq key gives 1000 calls/day **on each** of `gpt-oss-20b`, `qwen3.8-27b`, and `gpt-oss-120b` — three separate budgets. And freelm accepts **multiple keys in one variable** (`GROQ_API_KEY="key1,key2"`), rotating and benching them independently. That directly answers "exhausted right away": you get `models × keys` worth of headroom before anything runs dry, and when one model hits its cap freelm benches *only that model* and moves on.

## What I changed

1. **Provider-diverse chain**: Groq models → Gemini models → keyless OVH/Kilo as backstop. One exhausted key can't stall a mission.
2. **Auto-detection**: if any key is present, it leads with the keyed chain; with no keys it leads with keyless (so keyed IDs don't 404 on the free tier).
3. **`FORGE_MODEL_CHAIN`** still overrides everything.
4. **New tool**: `node scripts/model-key-check.mjs` — shows which providers are configured (counts only, **never prints keys**) and proves each model answers.

## Verified

- Key detection: `GROQ_API_KEY="dummy1,dummy2"` + `GEMINI_API_KEY="dummyg"` → detected `groq 2 key(s)`, `google 1 key(s)`.
- Routing: a dummy Groq key sent `openai/gpt-oss-20b` to **groq** (401, expected) instead of OVH — proving a real key will be used — then auto-fell back to keyless.
- `LINT=0`, **32 tests pass / 0 fail**.

## What I need from you

Add these as secrets (I'll never print the values):
- **`GROQ_API_KEY`** — your Groq key. Want more headroom? `GROQ_API_KEY="key1,key2"`.
- **`GEMINI_API_KEY`** — the existing Gemini key (aliases `GOOGLE_API_KEY` / `GOOGLE_AI_STUDIO_KEY` work too).

Then run `node scripts/model-key-check.mjs` and you'll see each provider pass/fail per model.

One thing worth confirming, because it decides the outcome: **is your Gemini key an AI Studio key** (starts with `AIza`, works with this path) **or a Vertex AI key**? If it's Vertex, freelm won't use it and I'd wire the `@google/genai` path instead.

7. **Copy the key immediately** and paste it somewhere temporary (a password manager is ideal). Groq shows it **only once** — if you lose it, you have to create a new one. It starts with `gsk_`.
8. (Optional, more headroom) Repeat steps 5–7 to create a **second** key. You can later use both as `"key1,key2"` to double the rate budget.

## ⚠️ First: revoke that key

That `AQ.Ab8RN6...` value is now in this chat, which means it's in the conversation log. **Revoke it immediately** and create a fresh one:

- AI Studio keys page: **https://aistudio.google.com/apikey** → find the key → **Delete/Revoke**.
- If it was created in Google Cloud Console instead: **https://console.cloud.google.com/apis/credentials** → delete it there.

I won't repeat or use the value. But assume it's burned — don't reuse it.

## What you've run into: the `AQ.` vs `AIza` change

Your key starts with **`AQ.`**, not the classic `AIza`. This is a real thing Google changed — and it's the likely reason your Gemini secret didn't take in CI.

- Google is moving from legacy **`AIza` "Traffic keys"** to new **`AQ.` "Authentication Keys"**, and AI Studio now issues **only** `AQ.` keys.
- There's a **known, widespread bug**: `AQ.` keys frequently return **HTTP 401 `UNAUTHENTICATED` / `ACCESS_TOKEN_TYPE_UNSUPPORTED`** — "Expected OAuth 2 access token" — when used with the documented `x-goog-api-key` header (exactly the header in your snippet) *and* with the `@google/genai` SDK. Many users across different projects report the same thing. Google has acknowledged it and asks people to submit a compatibility form.

So it's **not your setup** — it's Google's key rollout.

## How to tell if *your* fresh key actually works

Run your own snippet against a fresh key and read the result:

```bash
curl "https://generativelanguage.googleapis.com/v1beta/models/gemini-flash-latest:generateContent" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -H 'X-goog-api-key: YOUR_NEW_KEY' \
  -X POST \
  -d '{"contents":[{"parts":[{"text":"say OK"}]}]}'
```

- **Works** → JSON with a `"candidates"` field. Good, add it as a secret.
- **`401 UNAUTHENTICATED` / `ACCESS_TOKEN_TYPE_UNSUPPORTED`** → the known `AQ.` bug. The key can't authenticate yet, no matter what we do on our side.

## Your options

1. **Just use Groq — it already works.** This is my recommendation. The CI run proved your Groq key answers in ~150ms with 1000 requests/day per model. Gemini was only ever the *fallback*, and the chain already falls back to keyless OVH/Kilo without it. **You don't need Gemini to fix the reliability problem.**
2. **If you want Gemini anyway:** try creating the key in **Google Cloud Console** (some accounts still get `AIza` there), or wait for Google to fix `AQ.` propagation. Test with the curl above before adding it as a secret.
3. **If you still add it and it's `AQ.`:** our integration uses freelm's OpenAI-compatible path (`.../v1beta/openai` with `Authorization: Bearer <key>`) — which is the *other* auth style from your snippet. It may behave differently, but the `AQ.` bug has been reported on both. The `Model Key Health` workflow would tell us in a minute.

## How to add it once a key works

**https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/settings/secrets/actions** → **New repository secret** → Name `GEMINI_API_KEY`, Value the key.

---

Bottom line: don't let the Gemini `AQ.` issue hold you up. **Groq alone already gives Forge fast, reliable, keyed inference.** Want me to just finalize on Groq (Gemini stays as an optional, auto-detected fallback for whenever Google fixes `AQ.` keys), and get PR #65 reviewed and merged?

## What I built: task-tiered routing

`generatePlanAndPatch` now takes a **tier**:

| Tier | Runs on | When used |
|---|---|---|

So the cheap, easy calls (read the task, produce a plan) stay on the free public endpoints. The key is only touched for the real code work — and if a free call ever can't answer, it escalates to your key automatically rather than failing.

# Part 1 — Why GitHub Models is "a different story" (and why I was too soft on it)

**The honest headline: GitHub Models is dead.** It was fully retired on **July 30, 2026** — over two months ago. The playground, model catalog, inference API, and BYOK endpoints are all gone, for every customer, including existing users.

- Retirement notice: https://github.blog/changelog/2026-07-30-github-models-is-now-retired
- Announcement: https://github.blog/changelog/2026-07-01-github-models-is-being-fully-retired-on-july-30-2026

So when I said earlier "adding GitHub Models is a separate, larger piece of work" — that was too gentle. The real answer is **you can't use it at all**. Anyone still calling that API gets nothing back. GitHub's own recommended replacements are **Azure AI Foundry** (paid) or **GitHub Copilot** (subscription).

**Why it *was* a bigger project than a Groq key** (in case you're wondering what the fuss was):

| | Groq / Gemini | GitHub Models |
|---|---|---|
| How freelm uses it | Already built in — just set an env var | **Not supported** — would need a new provider written into freelm |
| Auth | Simple API key | A GitHub token (PAT with a special `models:read` scope) |
| Endpoint | Standard OpenAI-compatible | Its own endpoint + its own model catalog |
| Rate limits | Per model, documented | Per *account/token*, and largely undocumented |

So "GitHub Models" wasn't one line in a list — it was writing and testing a whole new integration. And now it's moot. **My recommendation: don't build on it.** Use what's already wired and proven.

---

# Part 2 — What's actually happening right now

Here's the whole picture, plainly.

## The problem you started with
Forge's "brain" kept failing or crawling: it would pick a model that returned *nothing*, then grind on a rate-limited free tier for minutes. Slowness *was* the failure.

## The fix, in two layers

**Layer 1 — a reliable chain.** Forge now tries models in order, and moves on instantly if one gives nothing:

```
Groq (your key)  →  Gemini (your key)  →  keyless OVH/Kilo
```

**Layer 2 — task tiers (this is the new part you just asked for).** Not every call needs your key. So I split calls by difficulty:

| Call type | Tier | Runs on | Costs you a key? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Read the task, make a **plan** | `free` | keyless only (Kilo, OVHcloud) | **No** |
| Actually **write the code** | `hard` | Groq → Gemini → keyless | Yes |
| **Repair** a failed patch | `hard` | Groq → Gemini → keyless | Yes |

The `free` tier is built from a client that **physically holds no keys**, so it *cannot* spend them. I proved that with a dummy key: `free` still routed to OVH and never touched the key.

## What's verified right now
- **Both your keys work** — proven live in GitHub Actions (Groq ~150ms; Gemini answering).
- **`LINT=0`, 28 tests pass / 0 fail** — including a new offline test that proves the free tier is key-free.
- **PR #65 is open** with all of this: https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/pull/65

## The one-line version
> Forge plans on free models, codes on your Groq/Gemini keys, and never gets stuck because each step falls through to the next.

---

## Two things I want to flag honestly

1. **GitHub Models can't be used** — it's retired. If you want a second free source, the realistic options are the ones freelm already supports (OpenRouter free tier, Cerebras, NVIDIA) or just more free keys. Not GitHub.
2. **This is all still uncommitted to `main`** — it lives on the `forge/task-model-layer` branch and PR #65. Nothing merges without your say-so.

Want me to record the "GitHub Models is retired" finding in `COORDINATION.md` so neither ChatGPT nor a future session proposes it again, and then move on to merging PR #65?

I now have the full picture. Let me answer the architecture question directly — because "exactly like me" has a precise meaning, and it's not a small patch.

## What "exactly like you" actually means

I'm not a fixed pipeline. I'm a **loop**: I look at the world, pick an action, observe the result, and repeat until the goal is met. That's the whole difference.

Forge today is a **fixed pipeline**:

```
VALIDATING → INSPECTING → READING → PLANNING → PATCH → TEST → PR
```

It's a train on rails. It can *repair* within a stage (the anchor-repair and target-check loops), but it can't **decide what to do next**. "Exactly like me" means replacing the rails with a loop:

```
look → think → act → observe → (repeat until done)
```

The good news: **you already own every part.** You have the exact primitives I use:

| My tool | Forge's existing equivalent |
|---|---|
| Read files | `GitHubService.fetchFile` / `listRepositoryFiles` |
| Write/edit files | worktree apply + `applyExactEdits` |
| Run commands/tests | `TestRunnerService.runCheck` (isolated worktree) |
| Open PR | `GitHubService.openPullRequest` |
| Ask a model | `ModelService` (the shared brain I just extracted) |
| **Decide what to do next** | ❌ **this is what's missing** |

So the missing piece is not capability — it's **the loop and a tool interface**. Right now `executeTask` hard-codes the order. A loop would let the model choose.

## The honest design

**M2 — the agent loop (one new file, additive):**

```
while (steps < maxSteps && !done) {
  model.pickAction(goal, history, tools)   // "read X", "edit Y", "run npm test", "finish"
  observation = await executeTool(action)  // bounded, sandboxed
  history.push(action, observation)
}
```

- **Tools = your existing services**, wrapped in one uniform interface (`name`, `description`, `inputSchema`, `run`). No new GitHub/test/model code — just adapters.
- **Model picks the tool.** That single change is what makes it "me."
- **Reuse the guards you already built:** every action passes through `policy.ts` (readonly/sandbox/review/publish), runs in the isolated worktree, and every step lands in `mission-trace.ts`.

## The part I have to be honest about — "without limitation"

You can't have literal no-limits. If Forge runs a loop on a public repo with your Groq/Gemini keys and full write access, one bad step can push to `main` or leak a key — and there's no human in that loop to stop it. **Unbounded autonomy is exactly the failure mode your own `AGENTS.md` forbids** ("never push to main, never destructive actions without human confirmation").

So the honest version of "without limitation" is: **no capability limits, but a hard safety envelope.** Concretely:

- **No limit on *what it can do*** — read, edit, run tests, iterate as long as it needs, across repos.
- **Hard caps that protect you** — max steps, wall-clock budget, and `policy.ts` as the last gate (so it can't self-grant `publish`). These are not "limitations," they're the seatbelt.

That matches your goal ("works on complicated missions") without the catastrophic risk.

## Why this is a milestone, not a patch

This is a genuine rewrite of the *control flow* of `forge.ts` — the one thing you told me not to do broadly. So I'd do it **additively**: build the loop as a **new path** (`forge-loop.ts`) that reuses every existing service and guard, run it against the same acceptance tests, and only make it the default once it provably beats the pipeline. Nothing about the current engine breaks in the meantime.

**Acceptance tests (requirements-to-evidence):**

| # | Requirement | Test |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Model selects tools; loop reaches a goal with no fixed order | Multi-step mission that requires read→edit→test |
| R2 | Every action respects `policy.ts` | Attempt a `publish` action under `readonly` → denied |
| R3 | Bounded | Runaway loop stops at max steps/time, never hangs |
| R4 | Isolated | All writes confined to the worktree; no escape |
| R5 | Observable | Every step appears in `mission-trace` |
| R6 | Degrades | Model failure ends safely with a diagnosis, no partial PR |

## What you already have

## The design: "the sentinel" — a verifier agent

Instead of you clicking merge, a second workflow does what I do:

```
Forge opens a PR (forge/** → main)
        ↓
SENTINEL wakes up (separate run, fresh brain)
  1. run lint + build + tests        ← deterministic, must pass
  2. read the actual diff            ← model judge: is this correct & safe?
  3. check every claim vs evidence
        ↓
  ALL pass  → auto-merge to main
  ANY fail  → leave open, comment why, ask Forge to fix
  UNCERTAIN → hold (do not merge)
```

The critical word is **independent**. The sentinel must be a *different* run with a *fresh* context — never the same agent approving itself. That's the "reverse" you described: Forge writes, the sentinel judges.

## The one honest truth about this

A verifier is only as good as its checks, and a *model* verifier can be wrong — so I will **not** make the model the only gate. The order matters:

1. **Deterministic tests pass** (lint/build/test) — this is non-negotiable, and it's the real safety.
2. **Model judge approves** — an *extra* signal, not the sole one.
3. If the judge is unsure → **hold for you**, don't merge.

Plus: `main` stays revertible, and the sentinel only ever touches `forge/**` branches from you. That way, if it ever merges something bad, the blast radius is one revert — not a disaster.

## Why this removes the human *and* the limitation

You wanted two things that felt in tension:
- **No limitations** (Forge works on hard missions alone)
- **Nothing bad reaches `main`**

The sentinel resolves the tension. Forge gets **full autonomy** (it can push branches and open PRs freely), and the sentinel is the **gate** — so you never have to be the one checking. The human is *replaced*, not *removed*.

Two pieces, and together they equal "Forge like me, without you having to babysit":

| Milestone | What it gives you |
|---|---|
| **M2 — the loop** | Forge *decides* what to do next (autonomy) instead of following a fixed order |
| **M3 — the sentinel** | Forge's work is *verified and merged automatically* (no human gate) |

| S1 | Merges only when lint/build/test pass | Force a failing test → sentinel refuses, leaves PR open |
| S2 | Independent verification | Sentinel runs as a separate workflow with a fresh context |
| S3 | Never touches `main` directly | Assert merge happens only via PR, only from `forge/**` |
| S4 | Uncertain = hold | Feed an ambiguous diff → sentinel does not merge |
| S5 | Explains refusals | On failure, posts the exact failing check to the PR |
| S6 | Revertible | Merged commits are normal commits, revertable in one step |

- **Tests are the authority.** Lint/build/test must pass. A check that *didn't run* counts as **failed**, never a silent pass.

- **`main` can never be a merge source.** Only `forge/*` branches are eligible.
- **Auto-merge is off until you flip one switch** (`SENTINEL_AUTO_MERGE=true`). Out of the box it verifies and reports — it does not merge.

## Forge vs. OpenHands — the capability gap

**Forge's whole toolset is four tools:**

| Tool | Kind |
|---|---|
| `list_files` | read |
| `read_file` | read |
| `edit_file` | mutate (gated) |
| `run_check` | mutate (gated) |

**OpenHands (me) has, in this one session:** a real shell/terminal, a file editor, a browser, a task tracker, skills, sub-agents, web search/extract, MCP integrations, and PR tools. Dozens of tools.

That's the gap in one line: **Forge has 4 tools; I have ~20+, plus a real computer to run them on.**

### The four dimensions that separate them

1. **Tool breadth** — 4 vs. dozens. Forge can list, read, edit, and run an allowlisted check. It cannot search, grep, install, browse, or run arbitrary commands.
2. **Execution environment** — Forge runs in a **GitHub Actions runner** on one repo checkout and reads files through the GitHub API. I run in a **sandboxed container with a full shell**, so I can install dependencies, run anything, and iterate. Forge's commands are allowlisted (`npm run lint/test/build`, `npm test`, `npm install --ignore-scripts`) — mine aren't.
3. **Memory/context** — Forge has **none between runs**; the only thing that survives is git and `COORDINATION.md`. I keep a conversation, read `AGENTS.md`, and can delegate to sub-agents.
4. **Planning depth** — Forge's loop picks **one action at a time** with step/time ceilings. I keep a task list and can spawn sub-agents for parallel work.

### So how far, really?

If I had to put a number on it: **Forge is roughly 20–30% of the way to OpenHands in raw capability** — a narrow, capable coding loop versus a general agent.

But two things matter more than the number:

- **The gap is mostly intentional.** Forge is built to be *deterministic and phone-directed* — bounded steps, a policy gate, an allowlist, an independent Sentinel. OpenHands is powerful *because* it's unbounded; Forge is trustworthy *because* it isn't. In the safety dimension, Forge is arguably **ahead** of me.
- **The gap is closeable.** Most of the distance is tool breadth plus shell access. Adding a `grep`/`search` tool, a `run_command` tool behind the allowlist, and reading `AGENTS.md` as memory would each move it materially.

**Bottom line:** the distance isn't huge in *architecture* — Forge already has the hard part (a real think→act→observe loop with safety rails). It's mostly distance in *tools and environment*. Add three or four tools and it closes most of the gap.

Got it — "general agent" (your voice-to-text mangled it twice, but I follow you). Here's what I mean, and what else OpenHands can do. I pulled this from the live docs so it's accurate, not vibes.

## What "general agent" means

A **general agent** is one system that can handle *many different kinds of tasks* because it has a broad toolset and reasons about which to use — rather than being hard-wired to one workflow.

- **Forge is a specialist.** It does one job: take a task, edit code in a repo, run checks, open a PR. Four tools. One pipeline. Bounded on purpose.
- **OpenHands is a generalist.** Same agent can debug a flaky test, write a migration, review a PR, triage an incident, or research an API — because it has a real computer and dozens of tools, and picks among them itself.

Same core loop (think → act → observe). The difference is **breadth of tools and freedom to choose**. That's the whole distinction.

## What OpenHands can actually do — by category

**1. Execution (the "real computer" part)**
Full shell/terminal, filesystem, run any code, install packages, browse the web. This is the biggest gap versus Forge.

**2. Tools**
Built-in tools, **custom tools** you write, **parallel tool execution**, and **structured output** (typed, validated fields). → [Custom Tools](https://docs.openhands.dev/sdk/guides/custom-tools.md)

**3. Extensibility — how you teach it new tricks**
- **Skills** — reusable instructions/knowledge that load on keywords, file paths, org, or globally → [Skills Overview](https://docs.openhands.dev/overview/skills.md)
- **MCP servers** — plug in external tools dynamically → [MCP](https://docs.openhands.dev/overview/model-context-protocol.md)
- **Hooks** — block dangerous commands, enforce checks before stopping, inject context → [Hooks](https://docs.openhands.dev/openhands/usage/customization/hooks.md)
- **Plugins** — bundle skills + hooks + MCP + commands into one package → [Plugins](https://docs.openhands.dev/overview/plugins.md)

**4. Automations — running on its own**
- **Scheduled** (cron) and **event-based** (GitHub events, custom webhooks) → [Automations](https://docs.openhands.dev/openhands/usage/automations/overview.md)
- **Software Factory** — four automations chaining an issue through dev → review → test → merge → [Software Factory](https://docs.openhands.dev/openhands/usage/use-cases/software-factory.md)

**5. Integrations**
GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket, Azure DevOps, Slack, Discord, Linear, Jira, Notion, Datadog, Vercel — I have skills for each.

**6. Ways to run it**
Agent Canvas (browser control center), CLI, headless mode for CI, Cloud, Enterprise (self-hosted), or the **SDK** to build your own agents → [SDK](https://docs.openhands.dev/sdk/index.md)

**7. Real use cases the docs list**
Vulnerability remediation, automated code review, automated QA testing, incident triage, COBOL modernization, dependency upgrades, Spark migrations.

## Why this matters for Forge

You're not far off the *architecture* — Forge already has the loop. The gap is **tools + environment**. Three concrete moves would push Forge toward "general":

1. Add a **`search`/`grep` tool** (today it can only list + read).
2. Add **`run_command` behind the allowlist** (it's limited to lint/test/build).
3. Read **`AGENTS.md` as memory** (it has none between runs).

Each is small. Want me to **close that gap** — add search + memory to Forge's loop — or finish **wiring the loop into `forge.ts`** first?

## Forreyra — everything you need

Merged as **[#77](https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/pull/77)** (`e7c5102`), on top of the browser engine **[#76](https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/pull/76)**. Full guide lives in **`FORREYRA.md`**.

### Use it in one command

```bash
npm run forreyra -- https://example.com
```
```
url:   https://example.com/
title: Example Domain
elements:
  [0] a Learn more -> https://iana.org/help/example-domains
```

Chain actions left to right against one open page:

```bash
npm run forreyra -- https://example.com click:0 extract:IANA
npm run forreyra -- https://news.ycombinator.com extract:Apple
npm run forreyra -- https://duckduckgo.com type:1=playwright press:Enter
```

| Action | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `click:<n>` | click element `[n]` |
| `type:<n>=<text>` | type into element `[n]` |
| `press:<key>` | press a key (`press:Enter`) |
| `scroll:up` / `scroll:down` | scroll a screen |
| `extract[:<regex>]` | print page text (optionally matching sentences) |
| `view` | re-print the elements |

### Three ways to use it
1. **CLI** — the one-command flow above.
2. **Agent tools** — Forge gets `browser_open / view / click / type / press / scroll / extract / close` and drives them like a person. Enable with `FORGE_ALLOW_BROWSER` (on by default).
3. **Library** — `import { BrowserService } from './src/services/browser.ts'`.

### Setup
Chromium auto-detected at `/usr/bin/chromium`. Point elsewhere with `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE=/path/to/chrome`. Installed via `npm install`.

### Proof (live, just now)
- `npm run forreyra -- https://example.com` → title **Example Domain**, element listed.
- `npm run forreyra -- https://example.com click:0 extract:IANA` → clicked through to `iana.org/help/example-domains` and extracted its text.
- CI: **verify + sentinel success**, browser tests run against real Chromium, **107 pass / 0 fail**.

### One honest limit
The interactive-element set is the standard clickable/typable group (`a`, `button`, `input`, `select`, `textarea`, `[role=button]`, `[onclick]`, `[contenteditable]`), capped at 120 per view; page text is capped at 4000 chars; no persistent cookie jar across runs yet.

Next rung when you want it: **natural-language extraction** (type "get the price", not a regex) and an **MCP server** so Forreyra plugs into any client — that's the rest of the TinyFish-style mission.

## Shipped: **[#79](https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/pull/79)** (`ac2c042`)

`npm run forreyra:mcp` starts a **Model Context Protocol** server over stdio. Any MCP client — Claude Desktop, Cursor, VS Code, the OpenHands agent — can now drive a real Chromium session with the same look-then-act model as the CLI.

### Tools exposed (9)
`browser_open` · `browser_view` · `browser_click` · `browser_type` · `browser_press` · `browser_scroll` · `browser_extract` · `browser_screenshot` (returns a real PNG image block) · `browser_close`

Built on `McpServer` from `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` with zod input schemas, reusing `BrowserService` — no duplicated browser logic.

### Client config
```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "forreyra": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["tsx", "src/mcp/forreyra-server.ts"],
      "cwd": "/absolute/path/to/Firstborn"
    }
  }
}
```
Documented in `FORREYRA.md` §4, with `node scripts/mcp-client-smoke.mjs` to verify the connection.

### Proof (real, not claimed)
- `tests/mcp.test.ts` **spawns the actual server and speaks real stdio JSON-RPC**, then drives a real headless Chromium against a real local HTTP server — initialize handshake, `tools/list`, `browser_open`, `browser_extract`, `browser_screenshot` (asserts real PNG bytes), `browser_close`.
- **Interop with the official SDK:** `node scripts/mcp-client-smoke.mjs` connected via `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk/client`, listed all nine tools, opened `https://example.com` → *Example Domain* → `OFFICIAL CLIENT OK`.
- CI: verify + sentinel success, **113 pass / 0 fail / 0 skipped** — I pulled the job log to confirm the MCP tests actually executed.

### Where this leaves Forreyra
Four ways in, one engine: **CLI** → **agent tools** → **library** → **MCP server**.

Next rung for the TinyFish-style mission would be **natural-language extraction** — ask for "the price" instead of a regex — so the MCP tool can return structured data, not just page text. Want me to start there?

**[03:07:18] Watcher/agent:**

#83 merged (`1ebd7ce`). The three defects ChatGPT found are now closed and locked with tests. Now the big one — **Forge as a whole software company**.

Let me lay out a concrete design before I build, because "whole software company" has a few possible shapes and I want to build the right one.

## The company model

The key insight: **a role is just the existing hardened loop with a different mandate, toolset, and judge.** I don't need a new engine — I need an *org layer* on top of `runAgentLoop`.

**1. Roles** (`src/company/roles.ts`) — each a named employee with:
- a mandate (what it owns), a scoped toolset, a policy profile (read-only vs branch-write), and a brain (model-backed, or scripted/deterministic for tests)
- an output contract: it must hand the next role a *typed artifact* (plan, diff, test evidence, review verdict)
- The roster: **PM** (decompose the ask into requirements), **Architect** (design), **Implementer** (write the code), **Reviewer** (read the diff, find flaws), **QA** (run real tests), **Security** (adversarial checks — the ChatGPT role), **Release** (branch/PR), **Docs**.

**2. Teams** (`src/company/team.ts`) — a team is an ordered roster + handoff contract. It runs each role through the loop, passing artifacts forward. Same shape as `stages.ts`, but the roster is configurable and each role has its own brain and authority. Produces a `TeamRun`: artifacts + evidence + status.

**3. Competition** (`src/company/compete.ts`) — the "they can compete" part. Spawn **N independent attempts** at the same work item (different roles, strategies, or models), each producing a diff + evidence. Then **judges** score them.

**4. Judges / "teachers"** (`src/company/judge.ts`) — the graders that decide who wins:
- deterministic rubrics first: tests pass, lint clean, smaller diff, no security regressions, acceptance criteria met
- optional model reviewer as a tie-breaker
- **a winner is only ever chosen on evidence** — a claim without a passing test doesn't score. Ties break deterministically.

**5. Supervisor / "CEO"** (`src/company/company.ts`) — takes one goal, decomposes it into work items, assigns a team to each, runs them (optionally in parallel), then runs an **integration** pass that merges the winners and re-verifies the whole. Bounded by a company-wide budget (steps, runtime, attempts).

**6. Shared artifact bus** (`src/company/bus.ts`) — how roles "integrate together": a typed, append-only artifact log (same discipline as `COORDINATION.md`) so every handoff is inspectable.

## Why this is real, not a demo
- It **reuses** the hardened loop, so every role inherits the timeout ceiling, completion verifier, and symlink-safe tools I just merged.
- The whole org kernel is **deterministic and unit-testable with scripted roles** — no LLM needed to prove collaboration, competition, judging, and integration.
- Model-backed brains plug in at the role boundary, exactly like `ModelService.chooseNextAction` does today.

## Phased plan (each slice ships green)
1. **Kernel (no LLM):** roles registry + team pipeline + competition + judges + supervisor, driven by scripted roles. Fully unit-tested. *This is the foundation and I can build it now.*
2. **Real brains:** wire `ModelService` per role with role-specific prompts; deterministic fallback retained.
3. **Real work:** supervisor decomposes a goal; teams work on real workspaces/branches; evidence persisted; PR opened only for the winning attempt.
4. **Surface:** `forge company:<goal>` CLI + MCP tools + `AGENTS.md`/`COORDINATION.md` + an end-to-end test.

## One constraint to flag
There's **no LLM key in this environment**, so Slice 2+ can't be exercised live here — but Slice 1 and the judging/competition logic can be fully proven with real tests, and that's the part most likely to have bugs.

**Want me to build Slice 1 now** (the deterministic company kernel: roles, team, competition, judges, supervisor — all tested)? Or would you rather I start with a narrower roster (e.g. Implementer + Reviewer + QA competing) and grow it?

# Mission 1 — Forge audit against Smart Farm, run 20 times

I fired the audit **21 times** (1 test dispatch + 20 runs): **20 succeeded**, 1 hit a transient infrastructure failure. Every successful run is identical:

| Signal | Result across 20 runs |
|---|---|
| Job that ran | `run-readonly-audit` — **always**. `run-forge` was **never** triggered |
| Audited commit | `e6d6dfa` (54 tracked files) — every time |
| HIGH finding | "Gradle wrapper is incomplete" (missing `gradle-wrapper.jar`) — **20/20** |
| Model findings validated | 2–7 per run; 19 runs via keyless `kilo/nvidia/nemotron-3-super-120b`, 1 via `google/gemini-3.5-flash` |
| Target code executed | **No** — every run |

The consistency across 20 runs tells you this is **deterministic design**, not flakiness. Re-running can't change it.

## My final opinion

**ChatGPT's diagnosis is substantially correct**, and I verified each claim against the workflow, the issue, the job logs, and the artifacts:

1. **✅ Forge was skipped.** `forge.yml` has two mutually-exclusive jobs. A `forge-audit` label satisfies only `run-readonly-audit`. Confirmed in run #53's jobs: `run-forge = skipped`, `run-readonly-audit = success`. Re-triggering the same label can never invoke the agent.
2. **✅ The fallback is static-only by design.** `audit-runner.ts` uses GitHub's read API and never clones or executes target code. Its own report says `Target code executed: no` and `Build/test execution: NOT_RUN`. The green checkmark proves only that the static script finished.
3. **✅ The missing wrapper JAR is real.** The tree at `e6d6dfa` has `gradlew` + `gradle-wrapper.properties` but no `gradle-wrapper.jar`.

**Two refinements I can make sharper than ChatGPT's account:**

- **The wrapper finding is a latent hygiene gap, not a demonstrated build blocker.** Smart Farm's own CI is **green** (`Build Debug APK` ✅, `Smart Farm Unit Tests` ✅ on main) because both workflows use `gradle/actions/setup-gradle@v4` + `gradle assembleDebug` / `gradle test` — they never call `./gradlew`. So the wrapper can't be used as-is, but the documented build path is unaffected. ChatGPT hedged this as "potentially important"; it's better stated as "real gap, not a blocker."
- **The core problem is architectural, not just a mis-route.** The workflow has *no path* that runs the real agent (tools + execution) in a read-only sandbox. The audit is a separate, deliberately static script. "Give Forge its full audit capability" therefore means adding a **third mode**: run the real agent loop against a cloned target in a sandbox with no writes and no secrets. The company work below now demonstrates exactly that pattern is buildable.

Minor note: the audit job passes `GROQ_API_KEY`/`GEMINI_API_KEY` into the model call — that's why 1 of 20 runs used Gemini. It's read-only, so low risk, but worth knowing the audit env carries secrets.

**Bottom line:** ChatGPT is right that Forge never ran and that the workflow mis-routes audits. It slightly overstates the Gradle finding as a suspected blocker. The real fix is a sandboxed *executing* audit mode, not a label change.

- `team.ts` — captures **real disk writes** into the change map, and derives **QA evidence from the checks the loop actually observed pass** (never a self-reported claim).
- `brains.ts` — directive per-role prompts with an explicit required tool sequence.
- `scripts/company.ts` — `--target` (generated sample) and `--workspace DIR` for real runs.

## What Forreyra did

Forreyra opened `https://github.com/new` in a real headless Chromium. It landed on `github.com/login?return_to=.../new` — the **sign-in page**. Same result from the platform browser. So there is **no logged-in GitHub web session** in this environment; Forreyra can reach GitHub but cannot act as you.

## The precise root cause

Forreyra and the API together isolated it. The GitHub App installation on `madnessgate44-debug` (installation `170570309`) is granted:

| Permission | Value | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| `contents` | **write** | ✅ can push branches (that's why `company/main` works) |
| `pull_requests` | write | ✅ |
| `workflows` | write | ✅ |
| `metadata` | read | ✅ |
| **`administration`** | **not granted** | ❌ **cannot create repositories** |

That single missing permission is why both the REST call (`POST /user/repos` → 403) and GraphQL (`createRepository` → FORBIDDEN) return **"Resource not accessible by integration"**, while `git push` succeeds. It's not a token-expiry or scope issue — it's a specific, fixable permission gap.

Found it. There were **three** separate failures, and I've now verified each against the real runs. Here's exactly what was going wrong.

## 1. Why the audit came back with wrong/"stupid" findings

The read-only audit's **test detection was broken**. In `src/agent/audit-runner.ts` the test-path matcher was:

```js
/(^|\/)(test|tests|src\/test|src\/androidTest)(\/|\.)/i
```

That only matches a literal `test/` segment at the **start** of a path, so it misses every `__tests__` directory and every `*.test.*` file. I reproduced it exactly:

```
false  src/services/__tests__/integritySourceOfTruth.test.ts
false  src/services/teacher/__tests__/adaptiveLoop.test.ts
```

So the Amina audit (`run 38689466114`) printed, in its own **Checks** section:

> `[FAIL] Test-file inventory: No conventional test paths found`

…while the **same report's** model half listed the three real test files. **The report contradicted itself** — that's the "stupid things." Amina does have tests.

**Fixed** — `detectTestFiles` now covers `tests`/`__tests__`/`spec`, `*.test.*`/`*.spec.*`, `src/test`, `_test.go`, `test_*.py`. Verified live against the same Amina target:

| | before (`main`) | after (fixed) |
|---|---|---|
| Test-file inventory | `[FAIL] No conventional test paths found` | `[PASS] 3 matching test paths: …__tests__/integritySourceOfTruth.test.ts, …` |

## 2. Why you had to run a workflow yourself

Two workflows trigger on a Forge PR — **Verification and Build Checks** and **Sentinel Verification Gate** — and GitHub holds each bot-authored run at `action_required` *separately*. The approve step in `forge.yml` approved only the **first** held run and then `break`-ed.

On PR #86 that left: `38688775148` (Verification) → approved ✅, but `38688775214` (Sentinel) → **stuck at `action_required`** — needing your click. **Fixed** (approves every held run until none remain), and I proved it by approving that stuck run — it went to **`completed success`**.

## 3. Why the workflow you ran manually failed

You dispatched a **coding** run. That path runs `FIRSTBORN_KEYLESS_ONLY: 'true'`, and the keyless route returned nothing:

```

Error: Patch Validation Rejected: Empty patch: No file changes proposed.
Diagnosis: MODEL_ROUTE (HIGH) - The keyless model route did not return a usable plan or patch.
```

The coding path is **keyless-only by policy** — so it's fragile and fails like this. (The *audit* path isn't keyless; it gets Groq/Gemini, which is why audits work.)

- **6 of the 14 findings are wrong** — every one of them a "your dependency versions are invalid/outdated" claim.
- The model claimed `typescript@^7.0.2`, `vite@^8.3.0`, and `dotenv@^17.2.3` are invalid/outdated. I queried npm directly: **TypeScript latest = 7.0.2, Vite latest = 8.3.4, dotenv latest = 18.0.8.** Amina is *current*. The auditor's training data is stale, so it hallucinated "TypeScript 5.x is the latest," "Vite 5.x is the latest," and "known vulnerabilities."
- **The single most important fact was missed:** `typescript@^7.0.2` doesn't resolve against the real registry — only `7.0.2` exists as a prerelease tag. So Amina **cannot `npm install` at all**. The audit never said this. It reported "the version is outdated" (false) instead of "the build is broken" (true).
- **The verification panel caught none of it.** Two of the four verifiers rubber-stamped everything ("All findings are supported by the provided excerpts"), and one raised a *wrong* objection. The panel, whose whole job is to catch this, did not.

**Phase 1 — four lenses (each won its work item as attempt `#1`):**

2. MEDIUM — `server.ts` instantiates `GoogleGenAI` inline, mixing infra with route handlers. *(Fair.)*
3. LOW — duplicated `isArabic = language === 'ar'` logic across components. *(Inferred, plausible.)*
4. LOW — `src/services/storage/index.ts` barrel export obscures deps. *(Real, minor.)*

**Security Auditor (`sec#1`) — 2 findings. Verdict: one overstated, one good, incomplete.**
1. HIGH — "Exposure of GEMINI_API_KEY in server-side code." *(Overstated. `server.ts` line 16 reads `process.env.GEMINI_API_KEY` correctly — that's the right pattern, not an exposure. The finding is a hypothetical.)*

**Quality Auditor (`quality#1`) — 5 findings. Verdict: 2 wrong, 3 fair.**
1. HIGH — missing lockfile. *(TRUE — no lockfile at Amina's root. Genuinely useful.)*
2. HIGH — critical services lack tests. *(Partly fair — only 3 test files exist. But it's a coverage gap, not a "HIGH" bug, and the panel disputed its evidence.)*
3. MEDIUM — "TypeScript ^7.0.2 is outdated and unsupported, current is 5.x." *(WRONG. 7.0.2 is the latest.)*
4. MEDIUM — no integration/e2e tests in CI. *(Fair — `deploy-pages.yml` runs unit tests only.)*
5. LOW — "vite ^8.3.0 may be incompatible with React 19; latest is 5.x." *(WRONG. Vite 8 is current.)*

**Configuration Auditor (`config#1`) — 3 findings. Verdict: ALL THREE WRONG.**
1. HIGH — "Invalid TypeScript ^7.0.2, update to ^5.0.0." *(WRONG — and it would make Amina *downgrade*.)*
2. HIGH — "Vite ^8.3.0 exceeds supported range, pin to ^5.0.0." *(WRONG.)*
3. HIGH — "dotenv ^17.2.3 outdated with known security vulnerabilities." *(WRONG — invented vulnerabilities.)*

This lens is the worst offender: its entire output is stale-model hallucination.

Its architecture paragraph (React client + Express AI proxy, context-driven state, no service layer in the backend) is accurate and reasonable.

## The key

1. **Real audit, weak run.** The company genuinely ran; the *model* was the free keyless one, and its stale training data produced six false findings.
2. **The false findings all share one shape:** "your versions are old/invalid" — because the model doesn't know the current date. That's a fixable bug (ground version claims against the registry, or refuse to make them from memory).
3. **The panel is the weakest link.** Its entire purpose is to catch bad findings, and it caught 1 of 6, while one verifier actively defended a false negative and two rubber-stamped. A panel that confirms everything is decoration.
4. **It missed the one thing that actually matters:** the repo doesn't install. It found the *symptom* (`^7.0.2`) and mislabeled it.

So: not rubbish, not a fake — but this particular report would embarrass you if you shipped it as an audit. The honest one-line summary is **"a real auditor using a weak model that doesn't know what year it is, with a panel that isn't skeptical enough to catch it."**

**[09:49:27] Watcher/agent:**

You've put your finger on the real problem, and you're right that there's a way. Let me give it to you straight, because it's the most important thing in this whole project.

## The principle

You will never verify an audit by reading code. So stop trying. Instead, **make the audit prove itself in a language you can read** — plain English plus machine output that can't lie.

The version bug is the template. When the model *said* "typescript is outdated," that was an opinion you couldn't check. When I *queried the registry* and it said "latest is 7.0.2," that was a fact you could check with your own eyes. The fix wasn't "a better model." It was **replacing an opinion with a checkable fact.**

Do that for every finding, and you don't need to understand the code — you need to understand "did the check come back empty or not."

## What that looks like

Every finding should carry its own proof, written so a non-coder can read it:

```
Claim:  Missing lockfile
Check:  git ls-files -- '*lock*'
Result: (empty — nothing returned)
Verdict: CONFIRMED

Claim:  Stored XSS via unsanitized content
Check:  search the repo for "dangerouslySetInnerHTML"
Result: 0 matches
Verdict: FALSE — claim withdrawn
```

You don't need to know what XSS is or what `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` means. You need to see **"0 matches → the claim is false."** That's it. The machine did the understanding; you did the 10-second check.

## The rule that keeps it honest

**A finding with no checkable evidence is not a fact — it's an opinion, and it gets labeled as one.**

So the report splits into two buckets, and you only ever act on the first:

- **Verified facts** — each one backed by a command and its raw output. Lockfile missing, version invalid, a raw-HTML sink present or absent. These can't be fooled, because they're not opinions.
- **Judgment calls** — "this is tightly coupled," "this architecture is messy." Useful, but explicitly marked *opinion*. You read them, you don't trust them blindly.

Right now your audit mixes the two together and calls them all "findings." That's the trap. Separate them and the fog lifts.

## And you can spot-check without any tooling

Two things you can do today, no code:

1. **Click the search link.** For any finding, open the repo on GitHub, type the term it's worried about into the search box, and look at the number. "Says there's XSS → I searched for the HTML thing → 0 results → it lied." That's a 30-second verification anyone can do.
2. **Ask "what command proved this?"** If the audit can't answer, the finding isn't real yet.

## The honest limit

There's no way to be 100% certain without reading code — anyone who tells you otherwise is selling something. But you can move from *"I hope the AI is right"* to *"the machine-checked claims are solid, and I can spot-check any one of them myself."* That's the difference between trusting a model and trusting a ledger.

## What I'd build

The remaining work, in order:

1. **A security-grounding pass** (same shape as the version fix): an XSS/injection finding must cite a real sink, or it's auto-demoted. Kills the false HIGH you just saw.
2. **An evidence ledger** — every finding gets a `command → output → verdict` block, rendered in the report.
3. **A "Verified facts" vs "Judgment calls" split** at the top of the report, so you know which bucket you're reading.
4. **Clickable search links** per finding, so you can spot-check in one click.
5. **Rerun on Amina** and show you the two-bucket report.

That turns the audit from "a model telling you things" into "a machine showing you proof, with opinions clearly marked." Which is exactly what you need as someone who directs this without reading the code.

Want me to build it?

## 2. Tell me which scratch repo to use

**Link:** https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug?tab=repositories

- Look at your repo list. Tell me the name of the repo I should use for the live cross-repo test.

## 3. Add the `FORGE_DELIVER_TOKEN` secret

This is the token the workflow uses to push a branch and open a PR **on another repo**. The built-in `GITHUB_TOKEN` cannot do that — it only reaches this repo.

### 3a. Create the token
**Link:** https://github.com/settings/personal-access-tokens/new

- **Token name:** `FORGE_DELIVER_TOKEN`
- **Expiration:** 90 days (or your preference)
- **Repository access:** *Only select repositories* → pick the scratch repo from step 2
- **Permissions** (under "Repository permissions"):
  - **Contents** → **Read and write**
  - **Pull requests** → **Read and write**
  - **Metadata** → Read (auto-selected)
- Click **Generate token** and **copy it** (you won't see it again).

> Simpler alternative if fine-grained gives you trouble: create a **classic** token at https://github.com/settings/tokens/new with the single **`repo`** scope. Broader, but works everywhere.

### 3b. Add it to the repo
**Link:** https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/settings/secrets/actions

- Click **New repository secret**
- **Name:** `FORGE_DELIVER_TOKEN` (exactly this — capital letters and underscores)
- **Secret:** paste the token from 3a
- Click **Add secret**

---

## Then run the test

Once 1–3 are done, go to:

**Link:** https://github.com/madnessgate44-debug/Firstborn/actions/workflows/company-deliver.yml

- Click **Run workflow** (top right)

  - **goal:** what you want changed, e.g. `Fix the findings from the latest audit.`
  - **attempts:** `1`
- Click the green **Run workflow** button.

It will clone the scratch repo at its current commit, run its real tests, push a `forge/` branch, and open a PR there. Tell me when it finishes (or paste the run link) and I'll read the result with you.

## The four corrections (this is the valuable part)

**1. Item F is unbuildable, and it targets the wrong bug.** `ModelResult` has no finish-reason field, so there is nothing to check — the spec even anticipates this. More importantly: #96 was **not** truncation. The model returned complete, valid JSON and *chose* to write a 400-char file. So truncation-detection would not have caught #96. The mechanism that *would* have caught it is B + D + A, which the spec also asks for. **Do F as the honest note only; don't pretend it's a guard.**

**2. Item G's `&gt;` claim is false.** I pulled PR #96's title: it reads `...README.md: '> Verified by the Forge software company.'` — the `>` arrived intact. Decoding HTML entities is unnecessary and slightly dangerous (it would corrupt any legitimate goal that genuinely contains `&gt;`). **Do the env-var passing (real shell-injection hardening); drop the entity decode.**

**3. Item D's thresholds don't fire on the worst case.** The rule `deletions > additions + 20 AND deletions > 30%` **misses a pure-deletion edit** (`additions = 0` → `0 > 0+20` is false → no block). That's exactly the destructive case. Use `deletions > additions` as the third clause. Also note D's whole-file-delete carve-out: spec says block "modified existing files" — deleting a file entirely is not "modified", so it escapes D (my existing guard deliberately allows intentional deletions). Worth stating explicitly so it's a decision, not an accident.

**4. Item B has blast radius the spec doesn't mention.** `buildWorkspaceTools` in `loop.ts` is **company-only** — the Forge agent uses a *separate* `write_file` at `src/agent/tools.ts:319` (which already has its own `edit_file` at line 286). So B/C protect the company path; the agent path is untouched. That's probably fine, but it means "the pipeline can never destroy a file" is only true for the company path unless you also harden `agent/tools.ts`. Don't claim the broader guarantee.
