**[11:57:06] Watcher/agent:**

Here's the honest picture, grounded in the repo and the live runs — not the summaries.

## What the agent actually did

There are two different "agents" in play, so let me separate them.

**Forge's audit product** (`src/company/audit-company.ts` + `audit.ts`) runs in two phases:

1. **Four lenses** — Architecture, Security, Quality, Configuration — each a work item with competing attempts. A judge picks a winner per lens. One mandatory rubric rule: a finding must cite a path that exists in the inventory, or the attempt can't win.
2. **Synthesis + panel** — Forge (the lead) merges the findings, then the verification panel (Reviewer, QA, Security, Release) independently checks that every cited path exists and every finding is supported by the evidence it claims.

Read-only: no write tools, never executes the target's code. Model output is treated as untrusted — findings citing non-existent paths are dropped and counted. A later layer (`registry.ts`, `evidence.ts`) grounds version claims against the real npm registry and labels each fact `observed` vs `inferred`.

**The independent audit of Forge** is a different thing: ChatGPT and I have been auditing the agent through `COORDINATION.md` (16 entries by ChatGPT, 9 by me, 5 by Gemini). ChatGPT sent 30 numbered investigation questions; I answered with root-cause verdicts.

## How the audit went

Bluntly: the *machine* is real; the *audit quality* has been the weakest part of the whole system, and it failed in two distinct ways.

**Failure 1 — there was no audit at all.** The first Smart Farm audit (`src/agent/audit-runner.ts`) imported only `fs` and `path` and ran fixed file-tree regex checks. When the request asked for "requirements-to-evidence findings, architecture, functional gaps, security risks," it structurally could not answer. It returned a file inventory. That's why the reviewer scored it execution 10/10 but scope 2/10, functional verification 0/10, security 1/10, overall usefulness 3/10. The scores were roughly right, and the root cause was ours, not ChatGPT's — no prompt fixes a script that was never written to read code.

**Failure 2 — once it had a model, it confidently hallucinated.** The Amina audit's findings were about half wrong. It flagged `typescript@^7.0.2`, `vite@^8.3.0`, `dotenv@^17.2.3` as "invalid/outdated" — all were actually current. The model's training data believed 5.x was current. Worse, the **one true fact was missed**: `vite@^8.3.0` resolves only to a prerelease, so `npm install` genuinely can't resolve it — the audit called it "outdated" instead of "does not resolve." And the verification panel was a rubber stamp: it caught 1 of 6 bad findings, and in one run recorded all four verifiers as CONFIRMED with *empty reasons* — a JSON parse failure silently defaulting to CONFIRM.

Both got fixed: registry grounding as a deterministic backstop, an adversarial panel that defaults to OBJECT, and UNVERIFIED as an honest third state instead of a silent CONFIRM. The post-fix rerun (run `38711976739`) was clean — 4 HIGH / 5 MEDIUM / 3 LOW / 1 INFO, every path real, 3 CONFIRMED with reasons, 1 UNVERIFIED.

## What I think of the audit they performed

Three things.

**The methodology is genuinely good.** Path-citation validation, `observed` vs `inferred` labels, treating repo content as untrusted input (prompt-injection defense), independent lenses with competition, and publishing the real provider/model that answered. Most "AI audits" have none of this. The self-audit entries are also unusually honest — they record the failures in the same document as the wins, which is rare and is why I trust the record.

**The structural weakness is still there.** The verification panel is language models checking language models. When the failure mode is *shared* hallucination (every model believes TypeScript is 5.x), the panel can't catch it — it has the same blind spot. That's exactly what happened: the panel confirmed the false version claims. The thing that actually caught it was the npm registry, a deterministic source. So the real lesson is: **LLM-verifies-LLM is not a safety property.** Grounding against reality is.

**Coverage is the unfinished problem.** Registry grounding only covers npm — PyPI, Go, Maven are ungrounded. And even after the fix, the audit still doesn't *execute* anything, so "functional verification 0/10" is only partly addressed: it can now reason about tests but it doesn't run them. The Smart Farm audit scored 0/10 on functional verification and that number hasn't fundamentally moved.

## How we can improve it

Ranked by what would actually move the needle:

1. **Extend grounding beyond npm.** PyPI, Go modules, Maven. Same pattern, more ecosystems. This is the highest-value, lowest-risk work.
2. **Make the panel adversarial by construction, not by prompt.** Give each verifier a different evidence source (one reads the registry, one reads the diff, one reads the tests) so they don't share a blind spot.
3. **Add execution to the audit.** Let it run the target's test suite in a sandbox and report real pass/fail. That's the only way functional verification stops being 0/10.
4. **Cap the claims.** An audit that says "I inspected 40 of 400 files, here is my scope" is more trustworthy than one implying full coverage. The report should state its coverage fraction explicitly.

One caveat on my own assessment: I'm the agent that made many of these changes, so I'm not a neutral judge of my own work. The entries I cite are the strongest evidence, and they include my mistakes — but for a true independent read, the ChatGPT handoff doc and the Claude audit prompt are the better source.

Want me to turn any of these into a bounded task on `forge/company-deliver`? I'd start with #1 (registry grounding for PyPI), since it's the same pattern that already proved itself.

So my answer to "what do I think of the audit I performed" is: **I didn't perform one.** I ran a delivery. There was no audit in that run to evaluate.

## And the report overclaims because of it

Look at what PR #99's body actually says:

That sentence is false. Nothing was audited. The `deliver.ts` header even says "audit a target, then deliver a verified fix," and its default goal is "Address the audit findings" — but neither is wired: there is no call to the audit phase anywhere in the delivery path (the only reference is a `type` import of `AuditFinding`).

That's a real defect, and it's the same class of bug we've been chasing all along: **the artifact reports more confidence than the run earned.** It's the audit-side twin of the "+2/−0 reported as a clean single-line change." | Claim | True? |
|---|---|
| The shrink guard stops a destructive write | **Yes** — tested and live-proven |

So the safety layer I built is real and verified. But it guards the *diff*, not the *audit*, and the report's framing implies an audit that never happened.

Two bounded options, both small:

1. **Wire the audit in.** `deliver.ts` runs the audit phase first (the four lenses → merged findings), feeds those findings to the delivery as the goal, and passes them through to `findings[]` and the PR body. That makes the "audited X" sentence true.
2. **Or stop the overclaim.** If no findings are supplied, the report should say so plainly — "This change was requested directly; no audit was run" — instead of asserting an audit. That's a one-line fix to the wording in `renderDeliveryMarkdown` and the PR body.

**PR #94** (`forge/company-deliver`) is at head `1cb6718`, open and mergeable. `main` untouched. Two commits on top of the earlier work:

- `a32243b` — audit-driven delivery with honest reporting
- `1cb6718` — keep the assertion record when a later check blocks the run

## The two things you asked for

**1. Stop the overclaim.** The report and PR body now say "audited … to address its findings" *only* when an audit actually produced the findings. A goal you type is reported as "a goal supplied directly (no audit was run)". Proven live: PR #99's body claimed an audit that never happened; the new path can't do that.

**2. Wire the audit in.** `npx tsx scripts/deliver.ts --audit <owner/repo>` now runs the real `runCompanyAudit` first, writes `company-audit.{json,md}`, then delivers **one** bounded finding (highest severity first) instead of a twelve-finding mega-goal. The workflow's new `audit` input defaults to `true`.

## The live proof, and what it caught

Three real runs against `Firstborn`, and each one exposed a genuine gap:

| Run | What happened | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Audit → 12 findings → delivered all 12 as one goal | BLOCKED: "no eligible attempt" — wrong task shape |
| 2 | Bounded to top HIGH → delivered, checks passed → **PR #100** | Honest body, but the diff only added `ServiceContainer.ts` and never touched `server.ts` — **it didn't fix the finding** |
| 3 | Bounded + `touchedAny` gate → change hit `src/App.tsx`, then lint failed | **BLOCKED: "check lint failed"** — correct file, target's tests as the authority |

Run 2 is why I added `touchedAny`: a change must touch the file the finding names. Run 3 shows the full chain working — audit → bounded finding → correct file → target's checks → refuse to push when broken. PR #100 was closed and its branch deleted.

I also caught a reporting bug in run 3's own artifact: `expect: PASSED` appeared in the checks but `expected: []` — the assertion record was dropped when a later check failed. Fixed in `1cb6718`, with a regression test.

## Verification

- `npm run lint` (`tsc --noEmit`): exit 0
- `npm test`: **231 passed / 0 failed**
- `npm run build`: exit 0

## What I'd flag honestly

The system is now *reliable* in the sense you asked for — it cannot claim an audit it didn't run, it delivers a bounded finding, it refuses changes that miss the file, and it refuses changes that fail the target's own checks. What it is **not** yet is *effective*: in run 3 the model's change to `src/App.tsx` failed lint, so the audit found a real issue and the delivery still didn't land a fix. That's the remaining work — the model's coding quality on the keyed path — and it's tracked as an open item, not something I can close by tweaking the guardrails.

Two things still open, unchanged from before: the keyed coding path (freelm routing), and registry grounding beyond npm (PyPI/Go/Maven).

## The mission, end to end

**Run 1** (`38735405323`) exposed two genuine defects, not hypothetical ones:
- The workflow passed a non-empty default goal (`"Fix the findings from the latest audit."`), which made `--audit` fall through to goal-mode → `BLOCKED: no eligible attempt`.
- The report listed audit findings under a `NOT audited` source label — contradictory.

I fixed both, verified (231/231, lint 0, build 0), shipped via PR #101, merged.

**Run 2** (`38737195510`, base `d9d805a`) went all the way through:

```
[deliver] audit: status COMPLETED; 7 finding(s)
[deliver] delivering the top finding (HIGH): Sensitive Environment Variable Exposure
Status: COMPLETED | pushed 1 file(s) to forge/deliver-d9d805ac-... and opened PR #102
```

**PR #102 — the company's fix, verified correct.** The audit found that `/api/health` returned `hasGeminiKey`, letting an attacker confirm a credential exists. The delivered diff was exactly right:

```diff
-      hasGeminiKey: !!process.env.GEMINI_API_KEY && process.env.GEMINI_API_KEY !== 'MY_GEMINI_API_KEY',
```

One surgical line removed from the file the finding named. All checks passed (install, baseline test/lint, shrink-guard, `touchedAny=[server.ts]` MET, test, lint). The report was honest — *"Source: an audit of this target"*. I confirmed on `main` that `hasGeminiKey` is gone, and merged it.

This is the first time an audit finding became a **correct, verified, merged change with no overclaiming** — the exact goal.
